ParlaMint-DK_20150703130143	The debate is open. The first rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Dan Jørgensen from the Social Democrats.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703130215	Thank you. If you read the government base, you will see that on the last page you have to go right to the last page, but it also says that the government wants broad cooperation on good and sustainable results, that is to say broad cooperation. I am delighted because history has shown that we politicians create the best and the most sustainable solutions for the Danes when we work together across political divides. I think the Danes want broad cooperation and I think they expect it from us. Today, unfortunately, it is clear why broad cooperation is first mentioned on the very last side of the government base. Before the elections, we, the Socialists, reached out to broad cooperation in the field of foreigners. Today comes the answer in the form of a classic block policy: Foreign policy in Denmark must be understood not to be based on broad cooperation, to be conducted with the narrowest possible majority and to be agreed behind closed doors in the blue block. We want a different approach. We believe that there should be broad cooperation in the Folketing on Denmark's foreign policy; this will create stability and security of legislation. We would like to participate in a broad cooperation so that all parties that have the will can take a share of responsibility for Denmark's foreign policy and a broad cooperation where we also look at things in a broader context. There are two things that are important to us: Firstly, we need to be able to keep up, and secondly, you need to work if you come to Denmark. Let me say a little more about the two priorities. We must treat refugees properly and fairly, but we must also be able to keep up. We need a realistic approach to the challenges posed by a large number of refugees in Denmark. That is why we have carried out the first tightening of asylum policy for 12 years; and that is why we want further action. We want better protection of Europe's borders, we want to send more rejected asylum seekers home, we want to send more criminal foreigners to serve in their countries of origin, and we want to penalise foreigners who defy an entry ban in Denmark more severely. In Denmark, we demand that you contribute to society... everyone should do what they can. Therefore, we say that if you come to Denmark, you must work. We have proposed requirements that mean that asylum seekers should be in jobs and learn Danish from day one. If they do not come into ordinary work, they must contribute in other ways, e.g.: in a useful job. We are also ready to make new demands on immigrants on long-term cash benefits. Today, more than every other non-Western immigrant of working age is not in work, too many are on cash benefits for decades, and that is not good enough at all. A new effort is needed. The Socialists are ready to discuss how, with existing and new requirements, if necessary, we can ensure that much more immigrants get to work. Refugees must contribute to society. But we must also ensure that children do not grow up in poverty. We are concerned that the new integration service is now also good for integration. Because it doesn't integrate people if they can't afford basic necessities, and it doesn't integrate people if their children can't afford to go to football and don't get Danish comrades. Therefore, I do not think it comes as a surprise to anyone that we cannot support the bill. As I said, we would have liked to see a broad agreement on Denmark's foreign policy; I think the Danes had that too. I hope that the government will not have to complete the entire government base until they reach the last side of the broad cooperation. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703130614	There are no brief comments. Then I give the floor to the next rapporteur, Mr President. Martin Henriksen from the Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703130629	Thank you. For almost four years now, the Danish People's Party has been talking about the need to tighten up asylum and immigration policy. We have now reached the point where, fortunately, there is a majority in the Folketing that wants to do just that. I think it's really good that we got started. I think it is very reasonable that we are now proposing to actually take initiatives which will hopefully have the result that the influx to Denmark will now be limited. We have said from the point of view of the Danish People's Party, and I would like to repeat that here we think this is a good start, and we would also like to sign the agreement which the Minister has helped to negotiate with a number of parties in the Folketing. But even if it is a good start, it is only the beginning. We also have the assessment that more needs to be done. Then I would like to say about the rapporteur's speech, which Mr. Dan Jørgensen kept that I think it is good with broad cooperation, but ultimately what counts is the results. It is the content of the agreements that we conclude that is the most important thing. Now we've been talking for four years, now we're acting, and that's supposed to be the words from here. We look forward to further negotiations on further action in the area of asylum. That is what is needed.

It is believed that if you provide a small allowance for refugees who come here, fewer will seek asylum protection in Denmark. If we look at the effects of the start-up aid, which can be said to be very similar to the benefit now being reinstated, in the 00s, which the Danish People's Party also contributed to, there was virtually no deterrent effect, so no one can be proved. It was the case in the '00s that when the number of refugees who came to Europe increased, it also increased in Denmark, and when the number of refugees who came to Europe fell, it also decreased in Denmark. On the other hand, there was another effect that was very documentable: poverty among the people affected by the start-up aid. We also did so in 2002 that we went in and changed the asylum rules, and at the same time we introduced start-up aid, the ceiling on cash benefits and did a number of other measures. I believe that the overall effect means something. I believe that the policy that the Folketing decides to implement actually means something, otherwise we might as well put an end to the Folketing. ParlaMint-DK_20150703131737	Thank you very much. It is with great pleasure that I can stand here today as a newly elected MP and as a new integration rapporteur with my first legislative proposal. And it is with extra pleasure when I look at the content of the proposal, namely the fulfilment of the commitment of the Left to take immediate action in the area of asylum soon after the elections, because we believe that the area of asylum is out of control. We have the highest number of asylum seekers for 15 years and we receive a disproportionate proportion of the asylum seekers coming to the European Union. That is why we are now going to tighten up significantly. The bill we are discussing here satisfies part of the immediate action in the area of asylum agreed by the Left, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservatives on Wednesday. With the proposal, we are introducing a new integration benefit at SU level, which newcomers will receive in future rather than as today's cash benefits. At the same time, we introduce a 1,500 DKK language bonus. for foreigners who show a willingness to integrate and who pass a test at the level we call Danish Examination 2. The law is proposed to enter into force per year. On 1 September 2015, the bill will be effective for those who are looking for Denmark right now, as it takes approx. 150 days, from the time you apply for asylum until you have a decision in your hand. The aim of the proposal is clear: we want to reduce the influx of asylum to Denmark. And we know that the conditions of residence, including the amount of social benefits, are of great importance to the asylum seekers. The draft law leads to an increase in structural employment of 400 people and leads to public expenditure of just under 400 million. kr. And I am pleased about that, because it means that in future more foreigners will have a life with a job, and we know that is one of the most important ways of achieving good integration. At the same time, we are freeing up some funds that will be included in the negotiations on the second phase of job reform, which should take place in autumn 2016, where we can lighten the tax on labour. The previous government carried out more than 31 relaxations of foreign policy while sitting in power. Nowadays, the clean-up work starts after the mess that we see as the SR government left behind in the field of foreigners. We are pleased with that in the Left. We are pleased that there are now new buns on the soup in foreign policy, and we are extremely pleased with the agreement made by the blue parties on Wednesday, and we naturally support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703132902	I would like to start by saying that the Enhedslisten is extremely concerned about the consequences of this so-called integration service. I think that the name is deeply misleading, because there is absolutely nothing to suggest that this service will bring about better integration, on the contrary. With the law here, refugees and Danes, who have been travelling for a period of time, have a significantly lower performance to live for. The right wing has two arguments. On the one hand, they believe that this benefit will have a deterrent effect, that is, that fewer refugees will seek protection here in Denmark and, on the other hand, they believe that it will get more refugees into work. Let me start with that deterrent effect. Firstly, I find it quite embarrassing that the government and the right are reacting to the fact that since the Second World War we have not experienced so many people being on the run, among other things. from the brutal Islamic State of Syria, by trying to do everything possible to prevent those people seeking protection here in Denmark. You simply try to scare them away. Secondly, there is absolutely no evidence that the scaremongering campaign is working. In the '00s, the VKO majority introduced the startup aid. Nevertheless, the number of refugees who came to Denmark followed developments in the rest of Europe. This means that when the number of refugees increased in Europe, it also increased in Denmark, and when the number of refugees fell in Europe, it also fell in Denmark. The right wing's myth that refugees sit and study the rules on child and cash benefits and folk pensions before they sit down in a falling boat in the Mediterranean knowing that there is a great risk of drowning, of losing their lives, there is absolutely no evidence. Then there is the government's second argument, namely that these low benefits will get more refugees into work. The Rockwool Foundation has examined the consequences of the startup aid back in 2009, and it came to the conclusion that about 9%. More people got to work while the rest just got poorer. Not least the kids were hit. The Rockwool Foundation writes, among other things, and I quote: 'The conclusion is that there are pronounced social problems among the affected persons whose living conditions are characterised by relative and, for some, absolute poverty. Once tax and rent have been paid, recipients of start-up aid, regardless of their family situation, are left with an amount which makes it completely impossible to pay the costs in a Danish standard budget... ▪ What is achieved? Greater social problems are being created as a result of poverty. The government says that the purpose is integration. The stupidest thing you can do if you want to integrate is isolate people in poverty. That's what you do with this bill. It is no wonder that it creates poverty, because the price of winter boots and on food and on rent and on heat and on medicine and on cottage in elementary school and on diapers is exactly the same for refugees as it is for us who are born and raised here in Denmark. Even if you don't care about the people this is going to hurt, even if you don't care about the children who are going to grow up in poverty here in Denmark, I just have to say that it doesn't work if the purpose is integration, on the contrary. This creates more problems, and it is not just a problem for the individual refugee. This is a problem for Danish society as a whole. Then I've heard the right-wing say it's just the same as SU. That comparison just doesn't make sense. If you are at SU, you have the opportunity to have access to a student loan, you have the opportunity to work alongside, you have access to cheaper residences, you get a discount on public transport, etc. etc. The comparison with SU makes no sense. Another element of the bill here is that you have to be rewarded if you pass Danish exam 2, because then you get these approx. 1,500 kroner. more to live for. I agree that it is extremely important to learn the Danish language as quickly as possible. This is absolutely essential for integration. But there are huge problems with this language requirement. First of all, we all know that a great many refugees come with extreme trauma, which makes it difficult for them just to sit down and learn a new language. Secondly, we also know that those who come without training will have a much more difficult way of meeting this language requirement than those who provide education. And thirdly, as a refugee, you cannot go to a Danish language centre and say: I would like to go to Danish, level 2. The language centre must assess where to place you, and if you are placed in Danish, level 1, and many do, then you are not able to jump onto the two. That means that for a great many refugees, that means that you can pass a two and get $1,500. more a month, really not be an option. As I said, we in the Enhedslist are deeply concerned about the consequences of this bill. In fact, I also think that it is unreasonable that we should have the first reading before we have had a phase of consultation with the relevant parties. I think it is an injustice that does not suit the new government.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703134604	Thank you very much. The alternative will vote against this bill. It was women, in particular, who fell out of the labour market completely and who never returned to the labour market again. Thus, there is a risk that the integration benefit may unintentionally help to support a family pattern where the husband works outside, if he can find work and where the woman goes at home. ParlaMint-DK_20150703135132	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. We move on to the next rapporteur, Mrs Zenia Stampe from the Radical Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703135146	Thank you. It can hardly come as a surprise to anyone that we in the Radical Left do not support the proposal. The proposal, as several have already mentioned, has two objectives: to reduce the number of asylum seekers seeking to move towards Denmark and to improve the integration of refugees into Denmark. We do not think that some of the two objectives can be pursued with this proposal, and I would like to say a few words about that. Firstly, there are the number of asylum seekers. In these years, we see some of the greatest flows of refugees since World War II. We believe that Denmark as a rich and privileged country has an obligation to help in this very difficult global situation. We are doing this by contributing to the efforts in the neighbourhood, we are doing this by helping to develop those states that could potentially end up in conflict, but we are also doing this by accepting the relatively few refugees who are knocking on our door and who are entitled to protection. It is clear that Denmark cannot accept all the refugees in the world, but when the refugees or flows of refugees worldwide increase, we find it quite natural that refugees and the number of asylum seekers for Denmark also increase. The greater the need for help, the greater the responsibility to provide help. This bill, however, is an expression of the exact opposite mechanics. The greater the need for help, the more we close the border, the harder we slam the treasury in. We are against this. But we are also against it because we do not believe that it makes a difference. The fixing of benefits has no documented effect on the number of asylum seekers. On the contrary, the organisations that know something about this tell us that asylum seekers do not come to Denmark because of the benefits they come here because of some much more basic things. One thing, of course, is that they are fleeing from something that Ulla Sandbæk from the Alternative already mentioned, but they are also fleeing to Denmark because they have a respect for human rights here, the opportunity to send their children to school, the certainty that you can get medical help if you get sick. It is also welfare. Well, welfare isn't just about getting money from a bank account, it's about living in a safe and secure society. It is the opposite that refugees flee from, and that is what they can get here. It has nothing to do with the amount of benefits. Secondly, we are also very doubtful about the government's second claim that lower benefits will lead to better integration. Others have already mentioned the Rockwool Foundation, which did an analysis of this some years ago, and it showed that the start-up aid had a documented employment effect of 12%. So 12 out of 100 found work because of the startup aid. 30 had found one anyway, but the remaining 58 percent. sat back just more impoverished and more isolated than before. For the Rockwool Foundation's analysis also showed that the startup aid does not make it possible to comply with an ordinary Danish standard budget, even if you withdraw everything called leisure and transport. We do not think that this is a relationship we can offer anyone, and when I say that it is because it does not just affect refugees and family members, it actually affects all Danes who have been abroad for just 1 out of the last 8 years. We therefore think that this proposal is sadly unfortunate in all possible ways, both for foreigners and also for Danish citizens. We cannot therefore support the proposal.

There were even individual cases in the late 00s, just before the change of government, where the number of asylum seekers was increasing in Denmark while they were decreasing in the rest of Europe. This simply shows that there is no link between these benefits and the way people choose a country. The next thing is that I am really concerned about this proposal, because I think it will mean that some people who have fled will grow up in poverty. It worries me, both for these people, but also for Denmark's future, because what does it really do for integration, what does it do for their ability to become a part of Danish society in the future, what does it do for the children's opportunity to go to sport, to go to school? I can both worry about the children and their future, but I can also be worried about Denmark and the costs that later come when the people run around the street, because I am worried that that is what will happen when integration fails. Does it benefit Denmark? I don't think so either. So we are voting against, and instead of creating more poverty, we would like to engage in dialogue on how to combat poverty. Instead of punishing people to learn Danish, we would like to engage in dialogue on how we can provide incentives, positive incentives for people to learn Danish, perhaps intensify Danish education, so that there is more of it free when they come, because many of them actually just go home and wait for the next Danish lesson to come. ParlaMint-DK_20150703143132	Thank you. Most of it will be dealt with in a legislative proposal later today, but in reality it is two legislative proposals which are very closely linked. In the way in which the reimbursement system is linked today, municipalities have great financial incentives to do everything but get people back into employment again. Of course, we as a society cannot live with that, and therefore a broad majority in the Folketing has also chosen to change it. The total employment effort must, of course, work, and if we are to get people back to work, the whole system must move in the same direction. One of the things that has been really important to us is that we do not want a system where too many ties are placed on the method used. We have smart, talented employees who sit in every corner of Denmark and work with that, they know best. What we need to steer is the objective: does it work? Are we actually getting people back to work again? So what we would like to do politically is to steer after, of course, it has to work. What is then the idea of the new refund system is to give municipalities a clear incentive to get people back into the labour market as quickly as possible. This means that at the beginning of the unemployment period the municipalities receive a great deal of money from the state. When you get a little further in the unemployment period, the municipalities get a little less money, and even further down the unemployment period they get a little less again. The idea of this is, of course, to put massive pressure on the municipalities to actually provide the safety net that you as a citizen need so much when you have gone and become unemployed. So all the technology contained in this bill is intended to ensure that people get the best possible help to get back into the labour market when they have been so unlucky to become unemployed. That's why the Social Democratic Party can of course support this bill. I would like to greet the Radical Left and say the same on behalf of them.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703143950	The bill we are debating now is a bill that we are very much opposed to in the Liberal Alliance. It is a question of increasing the compensation between municipalities. For example, the level of expenditure in Hjørring today is 106%. of the estimated expenditure requirement. This means that when account is taken of demographic and socio-economic differences, Hjørring uses as a net recipient of equalisation 6%. more than average. At Frederiksberg, which is a net contributor, only 93 per cent are used. The compensatory mechanism, as it has developed, creates a very distorted incentive structure. Incentives for municipalities such as: Lyngby and Gentofte to pursue an offensive business policy are not very big. That does not mean, of course, that they cannot have any ambition to do so. They just don't get much out of it if they succeed. Now you change it, so that amount goes up to $93. A municipality like Lyngby, which today delivers 950 million. kr. to the compensation scheme, must now hand over 8 million. kr. extra, and Gentofte must deliver 10 million. kr. extra. Both these amounts will increase gradually. In the Liberal Alliance, we find it very difficult to understand why you see a need to go further down this road. That is why we shall be voting against the proposal.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703144139	Since there are no brief comments, we thank the rapporteur and go further in the rapporteur's line. The next one is from the Alternative, and that's Josephine Fock.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703144157	Thank you for the floor. As has already been mentioned, this bill is linked to what we are going to talk about later, namely Bill 183, and the proposals, as has already been mentioned, are aimed at changing the financing of social benefits, thereby increasing the economic incentive for municipalities to get unemployed in employment. In the alternative, we welcome if a new reimbursement system can help the employees in the municipalities to have a greater opportunity to assess what the individual citizen needs. The differentiated reimbursements have for many years been governing the efforts of the municipalities towards the unemployed, and this has led to decisions that are more economically than technically justified. It is therefore gratifying that this system should now be abolished. There are, however, several issues that concern us. The very rapid phasing-out of the reimbursement and the fact that the reimbursement after one year will be 20% mean that the municipalities will have a greater incentive for cash thinking and that even fewer citizens will be granted more long-term benefits, such as: early retirement and rehabilitation. In future, municipalities will receive a much larger proportion of the State's subsidy as a general grant rather than a case-linked refund. The alternative is concerned that in the future the money will not go specifically to get people into employment, as the municipalities can then choose to spend it on other things. We hope, of course, that the municipalities spend the extra money in the block subsidy on preventive work, but we are concerned that it may as well be that they are spent on other operational tasks or installations and that in this way there is no guarantee of funding for good employment efforts. We are concerned about the social consequences of a change in the financing of municipal expenditure on services and, although it is sympathetic to want greater activity and an improved incentive system for municipal action, we fear that the consequences for the very weakest in society will be great. We are not alone in this concern. There are several of the consultation responses from the spring treatment in which it can be seen that both the LO and the FTF and the Danish Social Advisor Association express the same concern, namely that a change will provide incentives for municipalities to push the weakest citizens out of public support. A change in the rates of reimbursement will therefore push the economy of the municipalities and mean worse conditions for those who are furthest away from the labour market. The alternative cannot therefore support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703144432	The changes to grants for reimbursement in the field of employment will have local economic consequences. Although the new reimbursement system is overall cost-neutral, this means that some municipalities will experience a loss and that others will experience a gain. This bill provides for an adjustment of the compensation system because the unintended effects of the changes are limited, both for municipalities in one and for particularly disadvantaged municipalities. This is done both in the temporary compensation scheme, which will limit the losses and gains of municipalities in 2016 and 2017, and by adjusting the compensation system in general. The draft law has already been thoroughly explained during the course of the debate, and it is a re-submission, so I will not repeat it in detail, but on behalf of the SF, give our reasons for supporting the draft law. SF is not part of the agreement on the reform of the reimbursement system which is the reason for this adjustment of the compensation system. The reform has a strong focus on strengthening the employment efforts of the municipalities through financial incentives to make an early effort, but has a battle side in relation to the municipalities that have many citizens with complex problems and long-term public support. At the same time, I note that there is agreement within the conciliation circle that the reimbursement restructuring should not have unintended burden-sharing effects on the municipalities. Therefore, adjustments must be made to the municipal subsidy and compensation system both in the short and in the long term. The negotiations the SF has wanted to be involved in and have an influence on. The SF therefore backs this proposal for adjustments to the equalisation system, because it can correct the inequality between municipalities that otherwise results from the reform of the reduction system. The proposal will mean greater equalisation, and it is in line with SF's desire to reduce inequality and ensure a Denmark that is linked and where there are opportunities and developments throughout the country. I know from my work as a municipal politician that employment can be a difficultly manageable area and that fluctuations can knock the bottom out of the economy of an already crowded municipality. Precisely because the SF is critical of the restructuring of the reimbursement system, we will help to ensure a certain level of compensation so that municipalities are not penalised for circumstances in which they do not themselves have any influence. This is a transitional arrangement for 2016 and 2017 until the financing committee has to come up with a longer-term and permanent bid for the adjustment of the grant compensation system, which could enter into force in January 2018. For this reason, it is important for the SF that the new system of reimbursement in the field of employment should not undermine the municipal economy, not least in the outermost regions. We are prepared to enter into the negotiations on the equalisation system and we support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703150607	Thank you. This bill reintroduces the housing job scheme in unchanged form with retroactive effect for the whole of 2015. I have recently met several master craftsmen who have had a difficult time because orders in the order books have not struck after the housing job scheme stopped. We also know that the scheme has had a beneficial effect on the scale of undeclared work. As in previous years, it must be possible to deduct DKK 15,000. annual per year; person. The deduction will be designed as an equational deduction, which means that the tax value of the deduction in 2015 will be up to approx. 4,440 kr. All in all, the draft law is quite simple. It reintroduces the housing job scheme in its unchanged form with retroactive effect, just as the Left has promised. The bill is also the implementation of the agreement on the housing job scheme for 2015-2017 concluded between the Government, the Danish People's Party, the Alternative, the SF and the Conservative People's Party. However, this bill only concerns 2015; the plan is that the scheme from 2016 and 2017 should be revised to support green conversion and increased sustainability. This year, the housing job scheme will be extended by the scheme known from previous years and the new scheme will come into force from next year. In addition, from this year onwards, more funds will be allocated to the tenants. From now on, they can apply from an even larger pool for subsidies for energy renovations and climate adjustments in tenant housing. For example: small repairs would in any case have been carried out, you just took the grant with you. There are fewer such workers who fall under the green scheme. This means that the job effect is increasing; the technical term is that the loss of dead weight is decreasing. Secondly, a number of tasks under the old system have been of luxury. It could be a new kitchen without significant energy savings, and naturally it is the best-off who have used it. The green projects, on the other hand, will often have the character of investments, which bring benefits in terms of less heating costs and fewer flooding in cellars. They will therefore be more relevant to smaller-income households. In addition, the tenants have on average lower income than the owners, and they benefit more from the new model. SF is very concerned with green conversion • 40%. of our CO2 We think it is a much better way than to give top tax relief or blindly to provide business tax relief. So we support the scheme and we look very much forward to seeing it come into force. It will mean a great deal, and it will also have a much higher social profile than the old system; it was among other things. Therefore, we scrapped the old scheme. The SF supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703162510	Thank you. In the Danish People's Party we can also support the proposal, and it is for the simple reason that one has to adjust the rules in relation to the interest rate that one gets for the money that one has put into one's tax account, where the situation today has become the very strange thing that one can in principle get a negative interest rate when putting money into the bank. This means that people have deliberately speculated or may speculate on depositing money into their tax account, and so that we have to adjust the rules for it. However, we have had the debate just before the elections, where the Danish People's Party clearly indicated that we can support the proposal, and we can of course do so this time too.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162601	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Louise Schack Elholm, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162613	This bill, as several have already mentioned, is a re-submission of L 190 from the previous session, which was not adopted before the elections. The bill must correct the inappropriate use of SKAT, as it has been found that there is a dull tendency for some companies to pay too much tax because the remuneration of SKAT is more attractive than in a regular financial institution. Of course, it is not okay to speculate that the government should give companies a better return than the banks, and therefore the interest rates of the SKAT must be adjusted to reflect the current reality. This bill also introduces a cap on deposits of DKK 200,000, and the return on deposits in the tax account up to DKK 5,000. are deleted. Businesses need to invest their cash either directly or indirectly through banks or financial institutions. When funds are placed in banks rather than in SKAT, this will also lead to more financial stability, and this is certainly needed. Against this background, the Left supports the bill. I am going to say on behalf of the Conservative People's Party that the Conservative People's Party also supports the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162724	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next speaker is the rapporteur. Pelle Dragsted, Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162731	Thank you. Of course, the Enhedslisten also supports this proposal, as we did when it was dealt with under the previous government. Of course, companies should not have an opportunity to think that by paying too much tax they can obtain a higher interest rate than if they put the money in a bank. SKAT and thus all of us should not have to pay for this kind of benefit to the companies. That possibility of speculation must, of course, be stopped, and it is good that the new government is following up on this, and for this it deserves praise. When that praise is then given, I would now like to take this opportunity to wonder a little at the fact that another initiative which the previous government had taken to stop tax speculation will be rolled back. It is the case that for many years an artificially low value of the unlisted shares has been established, i.e. shares which have not been admitted to the stock exchange and valued there at a known price when handed over as a gift or as an inheritance. It has been possible to establish a so-called asset tax rate so that these unlisted shares are valued at less than they are worth, and thus it has been possible to get rid of cheaper taxes. It was a gap which the previous government took the initiative to close together with the Enhedslisten, but which the new government now wants to reinstate according to the government basis. So you want to open another tax hole, which could cost hundreds of millions of dollars to taxpayers, citizens, to those who are fortunate enough to inherit a bunch of shares. So I'd like to hear, when we're gonna have the tax minister come up here later in the day, why you close a tax mug, but at the same time you promise to open another one. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162909	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next speaker is the rapporteur for the Liberal Alliance, Mrs Merete Riisager. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703162917	The proposal amends the interest rates in the account tax system. This is done to counteract the situation where it may be attractive for companies to place excess liquidity on the tax account because the risk-free interest rates are currently extremely low. Some people experience direct negative interest rates for deposits with banks. The Liberal Alliance does not believe that taxpayers should provide a subsidy for companies to obtain a higher interest rate than the market rate by having a large amount in the tax account with SKAT. That is why we support this proposal. Current legislation has been drawn up at a time when the current low level of interest rates was unthinkable. Negative interest rates are historically not something that has had to be considered, and therefore the legislation has not been geared at all to the rather special situation. It is therefore healthy and natural to update the legislation on current conditions so that the tax account cannot be used as hidden business support. The Liberal Alliance is in favour of good business environments, including flexible and well-functioning tax settlement, but of course it can never be taxpayers who have to recoup companies' surplus funds for conditions that are better than market conditions. It seems unreasonable for companies that pay too much in tax to get less back than they have paid too much. But that is the market situation, and we have to adapt to that.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703164008	Thank you. The Danish People's Party wants it to be less attractive to apply for asylum in Denmark. There are, of course, many factors which affect where asylum seekers are going, among other things. the situation in the world, but also, to a large extent, the asylum practices, the asylum rules and the economic conditions in each country. This bill concerns the re-establishment of the principle of the right to family allowances and the provision of children and young people for refugees. The principle of remuneration means that entitlement to the full benefit is subject to the condition that at least one of the persons who are dependent on the child has resided or worked here in the Kingdom for at least two years in the last 10 years preceding the period covered by the payment. In short, reintroducing the principle of recovery means earning the right to benefits in Denmark, and later today we are also discussing a proposal that refugees should earn the right to a national pension. ParlaMint-DK_20150703164118	I do not see any brief comments, so thank the rapporteur. The next rapporteur, Mrs Louise Schack Elholm, has the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703164134	Thank you. This bill restores the principle of the right to family allowances and child and young people's allowances for asylum seekers. The Left has chosen to give asylum seekers a greater incentive to become part of Danish society. The government has therefore, together with the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservative People's Party, created an integration package. The package has three elements to give asylum seekers greater incentive to integrate into Danish society. The first is the introduction of an integration allowance, the second is the re-establishment of the principles of income support for family allowances and for children and young people's benefits, and the third is the harmonization of the rules on earnings for retirement pensions. This bill deals only with the second element. The acquisition principle was introduced under the VK government in 2010. Unfortunately, the previous government introduced a derogation for the profit principles of persons under Articles 7, 8 and 9b of the Aliens Act. This has meant that asylum seekers have received full children's checks from the first day of their residence permit. The previous government thus gave a marked increase in income to this group. With the restoration of the principle of earning, at least one of the dependents of the child must have been resident or employed in Denmark for at least 2 out of the last 10 years in order to receive full entitlement to family allowances or child and young people's allowance. It must pay to work when you come to Denmark. This is part of the background to the government's integration package. Our welfare system is based on working and paying taxes, and then you get the benefits that the state offers. As a newcomer in the country, Danish society has not yet been helped. At the same time, the package makes it less attractive to come to Denmark as an asylum seeker. Again, this proposal is only a small but important part. We must make integration work, and to do so we must ensure that we do not accept too many. Our country is under historical pressure of asylum and it is therefore necessary that we make it less attractive to come to Denmark. I noticed that the Socialists want broad cooperation with the government, but the Socialists themselves removed this rule by a narrow majority, since the Socialists had a majority in the last period of government. So it is no wonder that the reverse happens after a choice. However, I would like to say that I believe that the Left Government is endeavouring to create a broad majority. We have just dealt with Bill L 5 on the housing job scheme, which was a broad majority behind it, and what perhaps surprised me when we dealt with this bill was that I could not sense a sensitivity in the red block at creating a broad majority. I sensed more of a lynch mood against the parties that had grown up to form part of a broad cooperation. I hope that the Socialists will take a more constructive tone and that the other parties in the red bloc will take a more constructive tone so that it will actually be possible to work together across the middle. Let me conclude by saying that the Left supports the bill, and I must also say on behalf of the Conservative People's Party that, unfortunately, they cannot be here, but that they also support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703175230	Thank you, President. This proposal is part of the integration package that was made here shortly after there was a change of government. This part is about refugees and a national pension, and this is about comparing refugees with people who are already in Denmark. We have rules on fractional pensions, and they will also apply to refugees. In this context, it is normal to take into account the fact that you have to have a pension from the country where you come from. In cases where refugees cannot obtain it, they can then obtain if they cannot obtain the right to a retirement pension, integration allowance or cash benefits under the Act on Active Social Policy. We in the Danish People's Party think that it is reasonable that you should earn the right to a folk pension because the effort but has been made for Denmark is important, and that is what has always been the intention of a folk pension, namely that you have been a long time in the labour market and thus have also been entitled to a folk pension. That is also why we have the rules that you should not just come to the country, and then you can get a retirement pension even if you have not actually provided anything in Denmark. In this case of fractional pension, it has been done as it was agreed very quickly that resident refugees have the opportunity to conform to the changed rules. This will also provide for a transitional period to apply until 1 January 2021. This means that those refugees who do not have enough time to earn a pension before 2021 will still receive a pension under the old rules. After 2021, it is the new rules according to which you have to earn the right to a national pension that applies, otherwise you will receive integration benefit or cash benefits. In this connection, special supplements and personal supplements are available. The Danish People's Party considers it reasonable to look at the period of residence, that is to say the time spent in Denmark, and that is why we naturally support the proposal here, which is part of the agreement we have been involved in making.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703175507	There are no brief comments. We would like to thank the rapporteur and move on to the next rapporteur, Mr President. Hans Andersen from the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703175525	Thank you. I'd like to sign with Mr. Simpson. Leif Lahn Jensen, who would like to work together on a new future integration policy, I look forward to that. And then back to the bill. It is a legislative proposal which aims to harmonise the rules on earnings for retirement pensions. This means that refugees do not earn the right to a national pension according to more favourable rules than other Danes and other immigrants who have resided abroad. In concrete terms, this will mean the abolition of the right of refugees to include residence in the country of origin. Refugees who no longer wish to receive a full national pension may, on a case-by-case basis, receive personal allowances under the Pension Act, integration allowance or cash benefits. Refugees who wish to receive a fraction pension, like all other Danes and immigrants who receive a fraction pension, can also be entitled to housing support and can apply for other benefits, e.g.: health allowance and heating supplement. Recognition as a refugee in Denmark therefore no longer means that as a refugee you are entitled to higher benefits than others, and this is also true here in particular of the national pension. The Left hereby supports this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703193318	Thank you, President. This proposal must also be seen in the context of what we have been discussing earlier this day. ParlaMint-DK_20150703194425	Thank you for the floor. The purpose of this bill is reportedly to encourage municipalities to help the unemployed and the sick and people with disabilities into the labour market. But at the heart of this bill is a drastic reduction in the State's reimbursement of municipal expenditure on a wide range of labour and social services. This means that the municipalities themselves will pay 80%. of these benefits, where they have to pay somewhere between 35 and 70% today, depending on the benefit in question. This will, of course, lead to the municipalities tightening the screw even more against the unemployed and the sick, which there is no room for in the labour market. It is precisely the reality of a large number of municipalities that there is no labour market that will accept the long-term unemployed, long-term sickness and people with disabilities and other groups with reduced working capacity. It is not only on Lolland and on Langeland that you have that problem. Instead, the municipalities will do what they can to get people to lower benefits at the level of cash assistance, in resource stages, in job clearance or the raw cash assistance, or no benefit at all, whether these people can support themselves or not. This will all lead us to see even more examples of the unemployed being left to themselves on cash benefits without the offer of ordinary work or training which can bring them forward. We will see even more examples of municipalities forcing sick people beyond the edge by stopping sickness benefits, even if they are not healthy, by sending them in permanent work tests, resource sequences, flexible jobs of a few minutes per week, contrary to medical statements that citizens are probably just getting worse. These appalling consequences are clearly described in the consultation responses from the LO and FTF, the Danish Handicap Federation and the Council of Social Affairs Vulnerables. The LO and FTF writes, directly with a seldom sharp wording, that it can be expected that the restructuring of the reimbursement rates will create a strong economic incentive for municipalities to push citizens out of public support. It was a literal representation of LO and FTF's hearing response. This is a trend which can already be measured today as a result of the tightening of the rules on cash benefits, early retirement, flexible jobs, sickness benefits and cash benefits. It gets even worse with the implementation of this law. There are simply more people who become self-supporting in the harsh sense of the word, namely people who have no income at all, neither wage income nor transfer income. It goes without saying that the Enhedslisten is voting against this bill, and I should like to welcome it from the SF and say that they are doing so too, even if they may, I do not know, would have chosen some other words, but at least they are voting against it.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703194820	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. The next speaker is the rapporteur, Mrs Merete Riisager, Liberal Alliance. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703194827	Thank you. The bill we are discussing here changes the reimbursements to the municipalities when they allocate different transfer income. The model now being adopted is almost identical to the one proposed by the Labour Market Commission in 2009. It means that the system is substantially simplified. Instead of allowing the reimbursement to vary with the type of benefits and effort, one system is created as a starting point where the reimbursement is determined by the duration of the benefit. There is a need for changes in the reimbursement system because it is important that municipalities have sensible incentives. The proposal takes steps to ensure that the executive public authority bears more of its own financial consequences, and this is quite healthy. When it is the municipalities themselves that bear the responsibility, it could also be used as an opportunity to debureaucratize. There is no need to place a lot of detailed requirements on the effort when the quality of the effort directly affects the municipality's economy. At the same time, it is healthy that municipalities no longer have a great incentive for cash thinking. In the current system, municipalities are rewarded for pushing citizens from one type of service to another, while the new system alone will reward municipalities for getting people into work. It is a sound management principle to reward municipalities for achieving the goal rather than rewarding them for using certain means. In the Liberal Alliance we can easily see that we could go even further than the legislative proposal proposes, but since we believe that this is an important step in the right direction, we can support the legislative proposal.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703195006	Thank you. I see no one with brief comments. The next speaker is the rapporteur, Mrs Josephine Fock, from the Alternative. Here you go.

In the alternative, we fear that the economic incentives in the proposal will lead municipalities to increase the pressure on the weakest citizens of society. It is typically citizens who have complex social problems and who are therefore dependent on social benefits for a long time. The proposal may give black figures on the economic bottom line, but red figures on the social bottom line, and therefore the alternative cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150703195221	Thank you to the rapporteur, for whom there are no brief comments either. So the next speaker is the Minister for Employment. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150703195236	Thank you for your contributions and support for the bill. After all, the legislative proposal that we have now retabled and examined translates one part of the broad agreement reached by the Left, the Danish People's Party, the Conservative People's Party, the Social Democrats and the Radical Left on 2 February 2015 on a reform of the refund system and adjustments to the compensation system. The second part of the agreement has been discussed earlier today. After all, the reform is primarily concerned with a refund system consisting of a number of complicated rules and a bureaucracy that does not support a clear job focus and a focus on results. For some benefits, the rates of reimbursement are now falling over time, while for others they are constant. Some types of bets today give a high refund rate, while others give a low. Together, an incalculable refund system has been created where incentives to get citizens into work are not always quite clear. Instead, with this bill, we are introducing a simpler and more transparent system of reimbursement, whereby rates are uniform across benefits and falling over time. When all benefits receive the same rate of reimbursement, it encourages the municipalities to take action with the clear aim of getting citizens into employment without the distinction of reimbursement. At the same time, when rates fall over time, it is more worthwhile to take early action to prevent long-term unemployment. Overall, we are creating a clear financial framework for an employment effort aimed first and foremost at reducing the number of citizens who are currently on public services and who are of working age. I think that is a crucial improvement. A concern has been raised in the past that, with the reform, municipalities will choose to stop their efforts towards certain types of citizens, including those furthest away from the labour market. To that I would like to stress that it is an important priority for the government that as many citizens as possible should enter employment. That is why the new system of reimbursement gives municipalities a greater gain than today by getting citizens who have been outside the labour market for a long time into permanent employment. We need to focus away from the rates of reimbursement and to make effective employment efforts for the unemployed. In addition, it is part of the agreement that, together with the other parties behind the reform, we will follow closely developments in the level of activation and transition to employment. Thank you for the floor.
